### MAC-Tuning: LLM Multi-Compositional Problem Reasoning with Enhanced Knowledge Boundary Awareness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21773v1)
- **Authors**: Junsheng Huang, Zhitao He, Sandeep Polisetty, Qingyun Wang, May Fung
- **Abstract**: With the widespread application of large language models (LLMs), the issue of generating non-existing facts, known as hallucination, has garnered increasing attention. Previous research in enhancing LLM confidence estimation mainly focuses on the single problem setting. However, LLM awareness of its internal parameterized knowledge boundary under the more challenging multi-problem setting, which requires answering multiple problems accurately simultaneously, remains underexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce a novel method, Multiple Answers and Confidence Stepwise Tuning (MAC-Tuning), that separates the learning of answer prediction and confidence estimation during fine-tuning on instruction data. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms baselines by up to 25% in average precision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAC-Tuning (Multiple Answers and Confidence Stepwise Tuning), a novel fine-tuning method designed to improve the confidence calibration of large language models (LLMs) in multi-problem reasoning scenarios.  Unlike existing methods that primarily focus on single-problem settings, MAC-Tuning addresses the challenges of simultaneously handling multiple questions with a shared context. The method separates the learning of answer prediction and confidence estimation during fine-tuning.  The core idea is to first train the LLM to answer the multiple questions accurately, and then train it to predict the confidence of its own answers. Through experiments on a variety of datasets, the authors demonstrate that MAC-Tuning outperforms existing baselines, achieving improved average precision (AP) scores and reduced Expected Calibrated Error (ECE) in multi-problem reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on the *multi-problem reasoning setting* and the proposed MAC-Tuning approach. Most prior work on confidence calibration has centered on single-problem scenarios. The separation of answer prediction and confidence estimation is a reasonable and practical technique, given the added complexity of multi-problem reasoning.  The paper highlights the potential benefits of this separation, arguing that it enhances knowledge boundary awareness and reduces hallucination. However, this separation is not an entirely new concept, as similar ideas have been explored in other contexts. The novelty is mostly in the combination and application within the multi-problem reasoning framework.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem: the reliability of LLMs in complex reasoning tasks.  Multi-problem reasoning is becoming more common due to its efficiency in handling shared contexts, reducing API costs, and enabling more complex interactions with LLMs. By improving confidence calibration, the paper contributes to making LLMs more trustworthy and usable in these scenarios. The demonstrated improvements in AP and ECE are tangible and suggest practical benefits. However, the significance is somewhat tempered by the fact that the method builds upon existing techniques. The results show solid improvements, but not a paradigm shift. Also, the ablation studies and other experiments may not be comprehensive enough to convincingly prove *why* MAC-Tuning works as well as it does. Further analysis is needed.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the challenges of multi-problem reasoning and highlights the limitations of existing confidence calibration methods.
    *   Well-defined method: MAC-Tuning is a straightforward and easy-to-understand method.
    *   Solid experimental results: The experiments demonstrate improvements over baselines across multiple datasets, providing evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   Good ablation studies: The authors performed ablation studies on components of the MAC-Tuning method showing the contribution of individual parts.
    *   Addresses an emerging application area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental improvement: While the results are positive, the improvements over baselines are generally incremental, not revolutionary.
    *   Limited theoretical analysis: The paper lacks a deep theoretical analysis of why MAC-Tuning works and its limitations. More insight into the underlying mechanisms of hallucination reduction and confidence calibration is needed.
    *   Overclaims: Claims like "We are the first to explore LLM confidence estimation under the more challenging multi-problem setting" are not exactly accurate, and should be toned down.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential influence lies in drawing attention to the importance of confidence calibration in multi-problem reasoning and providing a practical method for addressing this challenge. It could spur further research into more sophisticated confidence estimation techniques and their application to complex LLM-based tasks. However, a deeper theoretical analysis and more substantial performance gains would be needed to have a more profound impact.

**Overall:**  The paper makes a worthwhile contribution to the field by focusing on an important emerging area and proposing a practical method with demonstrated improvements. However, the lack of a deeper theoretical understanding and the incremental nature of the improvements limit its overall impact.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### DeepSeek-Prover-V2: Advancing Formal Mathematical Reasoning via Reinforcement Learning for Subgoal Decomposition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21801v1)
- **Authors**: Z. Z. Ren, Zhihong Shao, Junxiao Song, Huajian Xin, Haocheng Wang, Wanjia Zhao, Liyue Zhang, Zhe Fu, Qihao Zhu, Dejian Yang, Z. F. Wu, Zhibin Gou, Shirong Ma, Hongxuan Tang, Yuxuan Liu, Wenjun Gao, Daya Guo, Chong Ruan
- **Abstract**: We introduce DeepSeek-Prover-V2, an open-source large language model designed for formal theorem proving in Lean 4, with initialization data collected through a recursive theorem proving pipeline powered by DeepSeek-V3. The cold-start training procedure begins by prompting DeepSeek-V3 to decompose complex problems into a series of subgoals. The proofs of resolved subgoals are synthesized into a chain-of-thought process, combined with DeepSeek-V3's step-by-step reasoning, to create an initial cold start for reinforcement learning. This process enables us to integrate both informal and formal mathematical reasoning into a unified model. The resulting model, DeepSeek-Prover-V2-671B, achieves state-of-the-art performance in neural theorem proving, reaching 88.9% pass ratio on the MiniF2F-test and solving 49 out of 658 problems from PutnamBench. In addition to standard benchmarks, we introduce ProverBench, a collection of 325 formalized problems, to enrich our evaluation, including 15 selected problems from the recent AIME competitions (years 24-25). Further evaluation on these 15 AIME problems shows that the model successfully solves 6 of them. In comparison, DeepSeek-V3 solves 8 of these problems using majority voting, highlighting that the gap between formal and informal mathematical reasoning in large language models is substantially narrowing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DeepSeek-Prover-V2 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeepSeek-Prover-V2, an open-source large language model for formal theorem proving in Lean 4. It utilizes a recursive theorem proving pipeline powered by DeepSeek-V3 to generate initialization data. This involves decomposing complex problems into subgoals, formalizing proofs of these subgoals in Lean 4, and creating chain-of-thought reasoning. The model is then trained using reinforcement learning. The resulting model, DeepSeek-Prover-V2-671B, achieves state-of-the-art performance on MiniF2F-test and PutnamBench. The authors also introduce ProverBench, a new formalized problem set including AIME competition problems. They highlight that DeepSeek-Prover-V2 demonstrates a narrowing gap between informal and formal mathematical reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of several techniques to improve formal theorem proving. The recursive subgoal decomposition using a powerful LLM (DeepSeek-V3) and the subsequent formalization using a smaller model is a reasonable approach to tackle the complexity and computational cost. The use of RL, informed by both formal proofs and chain-of-thought reasoning from a general-purpose LLM is also a positive contribution. The curated dataset, ProverBench, is a valuable addition to the community for future research.

*   **Significance:** Achieving state-of-the-art results on established benchmarks like MiniF2F and PutnamBench is a significant accomplishment. Solving problems from the AIME competitions demonstrates the model's capacity to tackle mathematically challenging problems. The paper demonstrates a step forward in neural theorem proving, particularly in bridging the gap between informal reasoning and formal proof generation. The open-source nature of the model is another significant contribution, enabling further research and development.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   State-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Effective combination of subgoal decomposition, formalization, and reinforcement learning.
    *   Introduction of a new formalized dataset (ProverBench).
    *   Open-source release of the model.
    *   Clear explanation of the methodology and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on the closed-source DeepSeek-V3 model for subgoal decomposition and initial reasoning data. While the release of Prover-V2 is open-source, the pipeline depends on a non-public tool.  This diminishes the reproducibility of the initial data collection.
    *   The ablation studies are limited. The paper could be strengthened by investigating the impact of different components of the pipeline (e.g., removing the chain-of-thought component, using a different initialization dataset).
    *   While ProverBench is a good start, more details regarding problem selection criteria for the textbook examples could increase confidence in the data diversity and generalizability of the models trained on it.
    *   The AIME competition problems results, while promising, are limited to solving a relatively small subset of ProverBench. This indicates that there is still significant room for improvement in handling these more complex problems.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings are likely to influence future research in neural theorem proving. The approach of combining LLMs for subgoal decomposition with smaller formal verification models may become a common paradigm. The ProverBench dataset is likely to become a valuable resource for benchmarking future models.

*  **Rigour and Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed, and the authors report detailed performance metrics on standard benchmarks. They compare against strong baselines, demonstrating the superiority of DeepSeek-Prover-V2. The authors use appropriate statistical methods and provide sufficient experimental details.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper showcases a strong performance boost and introduces valuable resources, its reliance on a closed-source model diminishes its overall novelty and impact. The significance of solving difficult mathematical problems on standard and novel benchmarks is undeniable. However, this work is an incremental improvement based on combinations of well-established techniques.  Therefore, I would not rate this work in the top tier.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Empirical Study on the Effectiveness of Large Language Models for Binary Code Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21803v1)
- **Authors**: Xiuwei Shang, Zhenkan Fu, Shaoyin Cheng, Guoqiang Chen, Gangyang Li, Li Hu, Weiming Zhang, Nenghai Yu
- **Abstract**: Binary code analysis plays a pivotal role in the field of software security and is widely used in tasks such as software maintenance, malware detection, software vulnerability discovery, patch analysis, etc. However, unlike source code, reverse engineers face significant challenges in understanding binary code due to the lack of intuitive semantic information. Although traditional reverse tools can convert binary code into C-like pseudo code, the lack of code comments and symbolic information such as function names still makes code understanding difficult. In recent years, two groups of techniques have shown promising prospects: (1) Deep learning-based techniques have demonstrated competitive results in tasks related to binary code understanding, furthermore, (2) Large Language Models (LLMs) have been extensively pre-trained at the source-code level for tasks such as code understanding and generation. This has left participants wondering about the capabilities of LLMs in binary code understanding. To this end, this work proposes a benchmark to evaluate the effectiveness of LLMs in real-world reverse engineering scenarios, which covers two key binary code understanding tasks, i.e., function name recovery and binary code summarization. To more comprehensively evaluate, we include binaries with multiple target architectures as well as different optimization options. We gain valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations through extensive empirical studies of popular LLMs using our benchmark. Our evaluations reveal that existing LLMs can understand binary code to a certain extent, thereby improving the efficiency of binary code analysis. Our results highlight the great potential of the LLMs in advancing the field of binary code understanding, and provide new directions for binary code analysis techniques.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study evaluating the effectiveness of Large Language Models (LLMs) for binary code understanding. The authors create a benchmark dataset consisting of aligned source code, natural language summaries, and decompiled pseudo-code.  They assess LLMs on two key tasks: function name recovery and binary code summarization. The study uses eight code-domain LLMs, eight general-domain LLMs, and compares their performance against four deep learning-based expert models.  The authors analyze factors influencing performance, including compiler optimization levels, few-shot prompting, pseudo-code length, and symbol information length. They also explore fine-tuning LLMs on a specific dataset and present case studies of real-world virus analysis. The results indicate that LLMs can understand binary code to a certain extent, especially for MIPS architecture. They also note that combining AI analytical power with engineering skills improves code understanding. The paper concludes with a discussion of future research directions and limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   **Strength:** The paper's strength lies in its comprehensive empirical evaluation of LLMs on binary code understanding tasks. The benchmark creation, while using standard tools, is a valuable contribution in itself. Systematically comparing the performance of different LLMs (code-domain vs. general-domain) against established deep learning-based techniques provides useful insights.

    *   **Weakness:** The methodology, while thorough, largely involves applying existing techniques (LLMs) to a new domain (binary code). The evaluation tasks (function name recovery, summarization) are also established areas of research. The degree of novelty in terms of methodology/approaches is somewhat limited.

    *   **Significance:** The paper's significance resides in highlighting the *potential* of LLMs for binary code analysis, especially for tasks that are traditionally handled manually by reverse engineers. This could lead to new approaches for automating aspects of reverse engineering, malware analysis, and vulnerability detection, as LLMs could potentially enhance security and efficiency in these fields. The comprehensive evaluation identifies the strengths and weaknesses of current LLMs, providing a roadmap for future research. Also, The study of parameters including the effect of architectures is significantly increasing the paper quality,

    *   **Missing detail:** The paper does not deeply analyze the error modes of the LLMs, or the *types* of summarizations and names that LLMs are best at. A more qualitative analysis of LLM outputs would greatly enhance the understanding of their capabilities and shortcomings.

*   **Rigorous Justification:**
    *   **Strengths:** The empirical methodology is sound. The use of well-defined metrics, comparisons against baselines, and analysis of influencing factors contributes to the credibility of the results. The inclusion of different architectures and optimization levels adds to the robustness of the study.

    *   **Weaknesses:** The ground truth generation for summarization using ChatGPT can be a weakness, since reliance on an LLM as a gold standard can introduce bias and may not reflect true human-level understanding and summarization quality. Also, The code used for this work could be provided for more repeatability and trust.

    *   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the field by providing a valuable benchmark, identifying promising LLMs, and highlighting important factors to consider when applying LLMs to binary code analysis. It also suggests future work that involves combining domain-specific knowledge, exploring new architectures, and improving robustness against obfuscation.

*   **Overall assessment:** The paper provides a valuable empirical study of LLMs for binary code understanding. While the novelty of individual components is somewhat limited, the comprehensive evaluation and analysis of various LLMs, across architectures, and against DL-based methods makes a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper is valuable for its comprehensive empirical work and roadmap for future research. Its contribution is meaningful but incremental, since there are no revolutionary approaches, and the ground truth is LLM generated. It also doesn't deeply dissect the *reasons* for failures. The influence will likely be in guiding future research on applying LLMs to binary code analysis, especially regarding the characteristics tested (architecture, obfuscation, optimization level, etc.)

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Why Compress What You Can Generate? When GPT-4o Generation Ushers in Image Compression Fields
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21814v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Gao, Xiaohan Pan, Xin Li, Zhibo Chen
- **Abstract**: The rapid development of AIGC foundation models has revolutionized the paradigm of image compression, which paves the way for the abandonment of most pixel-level transform and coding, compelling us to ask: why compress what you can generate if the AIGC foundation model is powerful enough to faithfully generate intricate structure and fine-grained details from nothing more than some compact descriptors, i.e., texts, or cues. Fortunately, recent GPT-4o image generation of OpenAI has achieved impressive cross-modality generation, editing, and design capabilities, which motivates us to answer the above question by exploring its potential in image compression fields. In this work, we investigate two typical compression paradigms: textual coding and multimodal coding (i.e., text + extremely low-resolution image), where all/most pixel-level information is generated instead of compressing via the advanced GPT-4o image generation function. The essential challenge lies in how to maintain semantic and structure consistency during the decoding process. To overcome this, we propose a structure raster-scan prompt engineering mechanism to transform the image into textual space, which is compressed as the condition of GPT-4o image generation. Extensive experiments have shown that the combination of our designed structural raster-scan prompts and GPT-4o's image generation function achieved the impressive performance compared with recent multimodal/generative image compression at ultra-low bitrate, further indicating the potential of AIGC generation in image compression fields.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of leveraging large Artificial Intelligence-Generated Content (AIGC) models, specifically GPT-4o, for image compression.  It proposes a framework that uses GPT-4o to generate images from compact textual descriptions (and optionally, extremely low-resolution images) instead of relying primarily on pixel-wise transform coding techniques. The core idea is to transform the image into a textual representation using a novel "structural raster-scan prompt engineering" method, which guides the AIGC model to preserve semantic and structural consistency during the image generation process. The method decomposes the images into a textual representation describing visual elements in a top-to-bottom, left-to-right order, capturing features, spatial relationships, and stylistic attributes. Experiments show the proposed approach achieves competitive performance compared to existing multimodal/generative image compression methods at ultra-low bitrates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using AIGC models for image compression is not entirely new, as the paper acknowledges. Several recent works have explored leveraging generative models, especially diffusion models and VQ-GANs, for similar purposes. The key novelty lies in: 1) the specific application of the *very recent* GPT-4o model, and 2) the "structural raster-scan prompt engineering" mechanism designed to preserve image structure and semantic integrity. GPT-4o's superior multimodal capabilities compared to prior generations gives the method a unique strength. The raster-scan prompting is a reasonable, though not groundbreaking, approach to guiding the generative process for compression.

*   **Significance:** If successful, this approach could have significant implications for image compression, particularly at ultra-low bitrates where traditional methods struggle. The AIGC approach might offer a way to preserve perceptual quality and semantic content better than traditional codecs at these extreme compression ratios. By decoupling the compression process from the pixel-level, this work explores a new avenue for image compression. The results suggest a promising direction, but the reliance on a proprietary and evolving model (GPT-4o) has drawbacks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Exploiting State-of-the-Art AIGC:** The paper takes advantage of the cutting-edge generative capabilities of GPT-4o, which represents a significant advancement in AIGC.
    *   **Structural Raster-Scan Prompt:** The prompt engineering mechanism is a clever way to guide the AIGC model and maintain consistency between the original image and the generated output.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves competitive performance compared to existing image compression methods at ultra-low bitrates.
    *   **No Additional Training:** The method does not require fine-tuning or additional training of the AIGC model, making it more accessible and versatile.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a Proprietary Model:** The method depends heavily on GPT-4o, a proprietary model, which limits reproducibility and long-term applicability. Future versions of GPT or changes in their API could affect the method's performance.
    *   **Lack of End-to-End Optimization:** The framework is not fully end-to-end optimized, which limits its ability to minimize the rate-distortion tradeoff.
    *   **Limited Dataset and Metrics:** Evaluation is performed on a relatively small dataset (10 images from DIV2K).  A more extensive evaluation on diverse datasets would be beneficial. More importantly, most metrics used in this paper are image quality metrics or CLIP-based consistency metrics. Further human evaluations (e.g. subjective A/B tests) of the reconstructed images are desired to further demonstrate performance gains.
    *   **Limited Analysis:** The paper lacks a detailed analysis of the failure cases and limitations of the proposed approach.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While the application of GPT-4o and the raster-scan prompt are interesting, the fundamental idea of using generative models for compression has been explored before. The incremental value is tied to the specific AIGC model and the details of the prompt engineering, making it somewhat less broadly applicable.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The work could inspire further research on leveraging AIGC models for image compression and other related tasks.
    *   The structural raster-scan prompt engineering technique could be adapted and applied to other AIGC applications.
    *   The insights gained from this study could inform the design of more efficient and effective AIGC-based image compression methods.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably novel application of a cutting-edge AIGC model (GPT-4o) to the image compression domain. The structured prompt engineering strategy is a useful contribution. While the idea of using generative models for compression isn't entirely new, the competitive performance at ultra-low bitrates, particularly without requiring additional training, is promising. However, the heavy reliance on a proprietary and rapidly evolving model, lack of end-to-end optimization, limited dataset, and somewhat incremental nature of the contribution limit its overall impact and justify a score of 6.5. The reliance on qualitative subjective experiments is also concerning. The paper could be made more convincing with stronger quantitative and human evaluations.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### 3D Stylization via Large Reconstruction Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21836v1)
- **Authors**: Ipek Oztas, Duygu Ceylan, Aysegul Dundar
- **Abstract**: With the growing success of text or image guided 3D generators, users demand more control over the generation process, appearance stylization being one of them. Given a reference image, this requires adapting the appearance of a generated 3D asset to reflect the visual style of the reference while maintaining visual consistency from multiple viewpoints. To tackle this problem, we draw inspiration from the success of 2D stylization methods that leverage the attention mechanisms in large image generation models to capture and transfer visual style. In particular, we probe if large reconstruction models, commonly used in the context of 3D generation, has a similar capability. We discover that the certain attention blocks in these models capture the appearance specific features. By injecting features from a visual style image to such blocks, we develop a simple yet effective 3D appearance stylization method. Our method does not require training or test time optimization. Through both quantitative and qualitative evaluations, we demonstrate that our approach achieves superior results in terms of 3D appearance stylization, significantly improving efficiency while maintaining high-quality visual outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for 3D stylization using large, pre-trained reconstruction models (LRMs). Instead of training or optimizing per asset, the method leverages the attention mechanisms within LRMs to transfer the visual style from a reference image to a reconstructed 3D object. By strategically injecting features from a style image into the later layers of the LRM's transformer blocks, the method achieves high-quality 3D stylization without requiring training or test-time optimization. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of this approach through quantitative and qualitative evaluations, comparing it against existing 2D and 3D stylization techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of pre-trained LRMs for 3D stylization, specifically exploiting their embedded attention mechanisms. While the concept of using attention for style transfer is well-established in 2D image generation, the adaptation to the 3D domain, and particularly within the context of LRMs, represents a novel contribution. Discovering the relevant attention blocks for appearance and geometry is also a unique finding. However, the direct adaptation of 2D methods to 3D diminishes this novelty slightly.

*   **Significance:** The method's training-free and optimization-free nature is a significant advantage. It makes the approach practical for interactive 3D generation and stylization pipelines, which are desirable in content creation workflows. The results presented show competitive or superior performance compared to existing techniques, further enhancing its significance. The method produces visually compelling results and offers a good balance between style fidelity and content preservation. However, the limitations highlighted regarding fine details and dependence on InstantMesh do temper the overall impact. Also, the work focuses primarily on *visual* style transfer, and doesn't venture into styles that involve changes in the underlying geometry (e.g., caricaturization).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Training-free and optimization-free: Enables efficient 3D stylization.
    *   Leverages existing pre-trained models: Reduces computational cost and complexity.
    *   Competitive performance: Achieves comparable or superior results to existing methods.
    *   Provides a good balance between style fidelity and content preservation
    *   The ablation studies are a strong element, demonstrating the importance of the design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dependency on the quality of the base reconstruction model (InstantMesh).
    *   May abstract or simplify fine details.
    *   Limited types of styles that can be transferred (focuses mainly on visual styles).
    *   The performance of the existing method depends on 2D Multi View Synthesis method Zero123++, so the results might change with a new MVS method.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of 3D content creation by offering a more efficient and accessible approach to 3D stylization. It opens up possibilities for interactive applications and real-time style transfer. Further research can build upon this work to address its limitations and explore more complex style transformations. The idea of probing pre-trained models to identify functionalities is also a valuable contribution to the community.

*   **Justification:** The paper's novelty, while rooted in 2D style transfer methods, is well-executed in the context of 3D LRMs. The significant contribution lies in its efficiency, making it relevant for practical applications. However, its reliance on InstantMesh and limitations in capturing fine details prevents it from achieving a higher score.

**Score: 7**

*Rationale:* A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field. The training-free nature and compelling results make it a significant advancement. However, the limitations related to detail preservation, reliance on an external reconstruction, and mostly adapting existing 2D approaches temper the overall score. Addressing these limitations in future work would likely increase the paper's overall impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### COMPACT: COMPositional Atomic-to-Complex Visual Capability Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21850v1)
- **Authors**: Xindi Wu, Hee Seung Hwang, Polina Kirichenko, Olga Russakovsky
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at simple vision-language tasks but struggle when faced with complex tasks that require multiple capabilities, such as simultaneously recognizing objects, counting them, and understanding their spatial relationships. This might be partially the result of the fact that Visual Instruction Tuning (VIT), a critical training step for MLLMs, has traditionally focused on scaling data volume, but not the compositional complexity of training examples. We propose COMPACT (COMPositional Atomic-to-complex visual Capability Tuning), which generates a training dataset explicitly controlling for the compositional complexity of the training examples. The data from COMPACT allows MLLMs to train on combinations of atomic capabilities to learn complex capabilities more efficiently. Across all benchmarks, COMPACT achieves comparable performance to the LLaVA-665k VIT while using less than 10% of its data budget, and even outperforms it on several, especially those involving complex multi-capability tasks. For example, COMPACT achieves substantial 83.3% improvement on MMStar and 94.0% improvement on MM-Vet compared to the full-scale VIT on particularly complex questions that require four or more atomic capabilities. COMPACT offers a scalable, data-efficient, visual compositional tuning recipe to improve on complex visual-language tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "COMPACT: COMPositional Atomic-to-Complex Visual Capability Tuning" addresses the challenge of improving multimodal large language model (MLLM) performance on complex vision-language tasks.  The core idea is that standard Visual Instruction Tuning (VIT) primarily focuses on scaling data volume but lacks sufficient compositional complexity in its training examples. COMPACT proposes a new data recipe where training data is explicitly generated to control for the compositional complexity.  The authors define a set of "atomic capabilities" and combine them to create training examples with varying levels of complexity (k=1,2,3). They show that training with a dataset generated by COMPACT, even at a fraction of the size of the full LLaVA-665K VIT dataset, can achieve comparable or better performance, especially on tasks requiring multiple capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the explicit focus on compositional complexity during VIT data creation.  While previous work has focused on scaling and/or curating data, COMPACT directly addresses the limitation of insufficient complex samples in VIT. The notion of breaking down tasks into "atomic capabilities" and systematically composing them is a valuable concept. The implementation of this concept through a data generation pipeline involving a LLM and quality control also demonstrates novelty. It goes beyond simply increasing the data volume, and instead optimizes the data itself for teaching more complex visual-language reasoning.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the data efficiency of MLLM training. It demonstrates that carefully curated and compositionally rich data can be more effective than simply scaling up the size of the training dataset. The substantial performance gains on complex tasks (e.g., MM-Vet, MMStar) using a relatively small dataset generated by COMPACT suggest a promising direction for future research. The focus on compositional complexity directly tackles a well-known weakness of current MLLMs and offers a pathway towards models that generalize better.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The concept of atomic capabilities provides a useful framework for analyzing and addressing the compositionality problem in MLLMs.
    *   The data generation recipe appears robust, with well-defined steps for sampling capabilities, generating questions, and verifying quality.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, showing strong performance on complex tasks with a data-efficient approach.
    *   The analysis of compositional complexity and the impact of instruction tuning ratio provide valuable insights into the training dynamics.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The data generation process relies on closed-source models (Gemini), which may introduce biases and limits reproducibility, although the authors commit to releasing the data.
    *   The analysis of atomic capabilities is somewhat subjective, although the authors do define them rigorously. However, the classification of questions into different complexity levels can involve ambiguities and could be subject to interpretation bias.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on visual-centric tasks, with less attention given to tasks requiring substantial knowledge or reasoning beyond what is directly visible in the image.
    *   The complexity metric, `k`, while useful for the study, may not fully capture every kind of compositionality, potentially overlooking other aspects contributing to task complexity. The authors themselves acknowledge the limitations related to data generation and knowledge-intensive tasks in the concluding remarks, which is commendable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on MLLM training by shifting the focus towards data curation and compositional complexity. It can motivate the development of more sophisticated data generation techniques and evaluation metrics that specifically target compositional reasoning. It also demonstrates that scaling MLLMs without considering the data distribution can lead to suboptimal performance, and that explicit modelling is needed.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

While the paper has some limitations regarding data generation and reliance on a complexity metric, its conceptual novelty, robust experimental results, and potential impact on the field are considerable. The explicit focus on compositionality is a significant step forward and offers a promising direction for developing more robust and data-efficient MLLMs. Thus, the paper is significant and deserves a fairly high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Report on the llms evaluating the high school questions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00057v1)
- **Authors**: Zhu Jiawei, Chen Wei
- **Abstract**: This report aims to evaluate the performance of large language models (LLMs) in solving high school science questions and to explore their potential applications in the educational field. With the rapid development of LLMs in the field of natural language processing, their application in education has attracted widespread attention. This study selected mathematics exam questions from the college entrance examinations (2019-2023) as evaluation data and utilized at least eight LLM APIs to provide answers. A comprehensive assessment was conducted based on metrics such as accuracy, response time, logical reasoning, and creativity. Through an in-depth analysis of the evaluation results, this report reveals the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in handling high school science questions and discusses their implications for educational practice. The findings indicate that although LLMs perform excellently in certain aspects, there is still room for improvement in logical reasoning and creative problem-solving. This report provides an empirical foundation for further research and application of LLMs in the educational field and offers suggestions for improvement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an evaluation of several large language models (LLMs) in solving high school mathematics questions. It assesses the models based on accuracy, response time, logical reasoning, guidance, and creativity. The evaluation uses a dataset of college entrance examination problems from 2019-2023, and involves several prominent LLMs, both Chinese and international. The methodology includes different evaluation methods for different question types (multiple-choice, fill-in-the-blank, comprehensive), and introduces a multi-round chain-of-thought prompting method to assess logical reasoning. The paper analyzes the results and discusses the strengths and weaknesses of each model in handling different aspects of mathematical problem-solving, and provides insight into LLM's potential application in education. It also explores different strategies to improve the model's performance like prompt engineering and providing guidance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the growing body of research on the capabilities of LLMs in STEM education. It provides a structured and detailed analysis of multiple LLMs, using a real-world dataset (college entrance examination problems), and evaluates performance on multiple dimensions that are relevant to practical educational applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper considers several key performance indicators beyond just accuracy, including response time, logical reasoning, guidance, and creativity. This multi-faceted approach provides a more holistic view of the models' capabilities.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described and well-defined, including the data selection process, the use of different evaluation methods for different question types, and the use of chain-of-thought prompting.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of each model in handling different aspects of mathematical problem-solving.
*   **Use of Leading LLMs:** The selection of LLMs are widely used, and some are leading ones, both locally and globally.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper focuses on high school mathematics questions, a relevant educational context, and discusses the potential implications for educational practice.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Methods:** While the evaluation is comprehensive, the methods used are not entirely novel. Much of the evaluation setup is applying known test methodologies.
*   **Scope Limitations:** While multi-faceted, there can be other factors that may be crucial when evaluating LLMs in education scenarios, like fairness, ethics, and personalization.

*   **Subjectivity in Certain Metrics:** Evaluating "creativity" and "logical reasoning," particularly for complex questions, relies on a combination of AI-assisted evaluation and expert review, which introduces a degree of subjectivity. While the expert review process helps, the subjectivity can still influence the results.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** The findings might be specific to the selected LLMs and the Chinese college entrance examination problems used. The performance of these models may differ on other math problems or in other educational systems.
*   **Lack of Comparison with Human Performance:** The paper lacks a comparison of the LLMs' performance with human performance (e.g., how well do students typically perform on these exams?). This would provide a better context for understanding the significance of the LLMs' performance.
*   **Lack of statistical tests:** It should have employed statistical tests to validate that the improvements are significant.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by thoroughly evaluating the performance of several LLMs on a practical educational task (solving high school math problems). It explores several important dimensions of performance, providing a more holistic assessment. However, the methodological novelty is relatively limited. There is room for improvement in generalizability by benchmarking against a broader scope and against humans. Overall, there is value in this research as it stands.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Fact-Consistency Evaluation of Text-to-SQL Generation for Business Intelligence Using Exaone 3.5
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00060v1)
- **Authors**: Jeho Choi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise in enabling natural language interfaces for structured data querying through text-to-SQL generation. However, their application in real-world Business Intelligence (BI) contexts remains limited due to semantic hallucinations, structural errors, and a lack of domain-specific evaluation frameworks. In this study, we propose a Fact-Consistency Evaluation Framework for assessing the semantic accuracy of LLM-generated SQL outputs using Exaone 3.5--an instruction-tuned, bilingual LLM optimized for enterprise tasks. We construct a domain-specific benchmark comprising 219 natural language business questions across five SQL complexity levels, derived from actual sales data in LG Electronics' internal BigQuery environment. Each question is paired with a gold-standard SQL query and a validated ground-truth answer. We evaluate model performance using answer accuracy, execution success rate, semantic error rate, and non-response rate. Experimental results show that while Exaone 3.5 performs well on simple aggregation tasks (93% accuracy in L1), it exhibits substantial degradation in arithmetic reasoning (4% accuracy in H1) and grouped ranking tasks (31% in H4), with semantic errors and non-responses concentrated in complex cases. Qualitative error analysis further identifies common failure types such as misapplied arithmetic logic, incomplete filtering, and incorrect grouping operations. Our findings highlight the current limitations of LLMs in business-critical environments and underscore the need for fact-consistency validation layers and hybrid reasoning approaches. This work contributes a reproducible benchmark and evaluation methodology for advancing reliable natural language interfaces to structured enterprise data systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of using Large Language Models (LLMs) for text-to-SQL generation in real-world Business Intelligence (BI) settings. It highlights the limitations of existing benchmarks and evaluation metrics that don't capture the specific complexities and precision requirements of enterprise data. The authors propose a "Fact-Consistency Evaluation Framework" using LG's Exaone 3.5 LLM and a domain-specific benchmark derived from LG's internal sales data. This benchmark consists of 219 natural language questions categorized by SQL complexity. The evaluation focuses on answer accuracy, execution success rate, semantic error rate, and non-response rate. The results show that while Exaone 3.5 performs well on simple queries, performance degrades significantly for complex queries requiring arithmetic reasoning or grouped ranking. The authors analyze common failure types and suggest integrating fact-consistency validation mechanisms for deploying LLM-based text-to-SQL systems in BI workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper tackles a highly relevant and practical problem: using LLMs for BI, moving beyond academic datasets to a real-world enterprise setting.
    *   **Domain-Specific Benchmark:** The creation of a domain-specific benchmark based on actual sales data from a large corporation (LG Electronics) addresses a significant gap in the existing literature, which largely relies on synthetic or academic datasets. This increases the ecological validity of the study.
    *   **Fact-Consistency Evaluation:** The proposed Fact-Consistency Evaluation Framework is a valuable contribution, shifting the focus from syntactic correctness (string matching of SQL queries) or execution success to the more critical aspect of semantic accuracy and factual alignment with business ground truths.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The detailed error analysis provides valuable insights into the specific challenges LLMs face in BI applications, such as arithmetic reasoning failures, grouping and ranking errors, and filter misalignment.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper presents a clear and well-defined methodology, making it easy to understand and reproduce the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the focus on LG's internal data is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings. The specific characteristics of LG's sales data and BI systems may not be representative of all enterprises.
    *   **Reliance on One LLM:** The study primarily evaluates Exaone 3.5. While this allows for a focused analysis, it would be stronger to compare the performance of multiple LLMs to provide a broader perspective on the capabilities and limitations of different models in this context. The selection of the model may also have a bias depending on the authors.
    *   **Modest Novelty in Evaluation Metrics:** While the Fact-Consistency evaluation is valuable, the paper primarily relies on standard evaluation metrics (accuracy, success rate, error rate). Novel evaluation metrics tailored specifically to the unique challenges of BI applications could have enhanced the contribution.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Solutions:** While the paper identifies common failure types, it offers only high-level suggestions for improvement (e.g., hybrid approaches, schema-aware prompting). A more in-depth exploration of potential solutions would strengthen the paper's impact.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The domain-specific benchmark is a novel and significant contribution, as it addresses a gap in existing resources for evaluating text-to-SQL systems in enterprise settings.
    *   The emphasis on fact-consistency evaluation is a valuable addition to the field, moving beyond traditional syntactic metrics to focus on semantic accuracy and factual alignment.
    *   The error analysis provides actionable insights for improving the performance of LLMs in BI applications.

**Justification:**

This paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a practical challenge in a realistic setting. The creation of a domain-specific benchmark based on real-world sales data, and the focus on evaluating fact-consistency, are key strengths. The error analysis provides useful insights for future research and development. However, the limited generalizability, reliance on a single LLM, and modest novelty in evaluation metrics prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The study primarily serves as an initial investigation in this space, requiring more in-depth experiments and exploration of solutions.

### Enhancing Security and Strengthening Defenses in Automated Short-Answer Grading Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00061v1)
- **Authors**: Sahar Yarmohammadtoosky, Yiyun Zhou, Victoria Yaneva, Peter Baldwin, Saed Rezayi, Brian Clauser, Polina Harikeo
- **Abstract**: This study examines vulnerabilities in transformer-based automated short-answer grading systems used in medical education, with a focus on how these systems can be manipulated through adversarial gaming strategies. Our research identifies three main types of gaming strategies that exploit the system's weaknesses, potentially leading to false positives. To counteract these vulnerabilities, we implement several adversarial training methods designed to enhance the systems' robustness. Our results indicate that these methods significantly reduce the susceptibility of grading systems to such manipulations, especially when combined with ensemble techniques like majority voting and ridge regression, which further improve the system's defense against sophisticated adversarial inputs. Additionally, employing large language models such as GPT-4 with varied prompting techniques has shown promise in recognizing and scoring gaming strategies effectively. The findings underscore the importance of continuous improvements in AI-driven educational tools to ensure their reliability and fairness in high-stakes settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates vulnerabilities in transformer-based Automated Short Answer Grading (ASAG) systems, specifically within the context of medical education. It identifies three gaming strategies that students could employ to manipulate these systems. To counter these vulnerabilities, the authors implement several adversarial training methods and ensemble techniques (majority voting and Ridge regression). The study also explores the use of large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4, utilizing prompt engineering to detect and score gaming strategies. Results indicate that adversarial training, especially when combined with ensemble methods, reduces the susceptibility of ASAG systems to manipulation. Furthermore, LLMs with proper prompting techniques show promise in recognizing and scoring these gaming strategies. The research underscores the need for continuous improvements in AI-driven educational tools to ensure reliability and fairness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a critical and timely problem: the vulnerability of AI-based educational assessment systems to manipulation. As ASAG systems become more prevalent, it's crucial to understand and mitigate potential gaming strategies.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The study provides a systematic analysis of different gaming strategies and countermeasures. The authors investigate various adversarial training techniques and ensemble methods, offering a comprehensive view of potential defense mechanisms.
    *   **Use of LLMs:** Exploring the use of GPT-4 for detecting gaming strategies is a novel and promising aspect of the research. The results suggest that LLMs can be effectively employed as detectors, potentially complementing adversarial training approaches.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have direct practical implications for developers and users of ASAG systems, highlighting the importance of robust training and defense mechanisms. The study also emphasizes the need for ethical considerations in AI-driven assessment.
    *   **Extends Previous Research:** The paper builds upon previous studies that identified vulnerabilities and quantified the impact of gaming strategies, by focusing on systematically evaluating multiple adversarial training techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulated Data:** A significant limitation is the use of simulated gaming strategies. While these strategies are plausible, they may not fully capture the complexity and diversity of real-world test-taker behavior. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** The study focuses on the medical education domain and a specific scoring model (ACTA). The generalizability of the findings to other domains (e.g., legal education, general writing assessment) and scoring systems remains uncertain.
    *   **Limited LLM Experimentation:** The LLM experiments, though promising, are based on a relatively small sample size (100 samples of each gaming data and real data), which limits the ability to draw firm conclusions about their effectiveness.
    *   **Overfitting Potential:** The gains observed from the adversarial training approaches may be, in part, the result of overfitting. There is a need for more rigorous experimentation to demonstrate generalizability over unseen examples.
    *   **Limited Novelty on LLM use:** While the LLM usage is promising, a more detailed analysis of prompt engineering techniques is needed. The results of the prompt engineering are not drastically different.

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a incremental contribution by systematically studying and evaluating various adversarial training techniques to enhance the robustness of ASAG in the medical domain. It also builds on previous related works.
*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses an important security problem for the next-generation education system. The study could help promote the use of these models in real-world applications.

**Justification:**

The paper demonstrates a solid understanding of the problem and employs a systematic approach to investigate potential solutions. The inclusion of LLMs is an interesting avenue for future research. However, the reliance on simulated data and the domain-specific nature of the study somewhat limit the broader impact of the findings. Also, the results are incremental.
