The feature importance and attention matrix analysis can reveal previously unknown dependencies, leading to improved reduced order models.

    *   **Machine Learning for Dynamical Systems:** The paper advances the application of transformers to the domain of dynamical systems. It demonstrates the effectiveness of TFT (and its extension) in capturing the dynamics of complex systems with time-varying inputs and parameters. This could inspire further research into the use of transformers for other dynamical systems problems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the iSTFT architecture and its application to dynamical systems.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the accuracy and effectiveness of iSTFT across different dynamical systems.
    *   The interpretability analysis provides valuable insights into the system's behavior.
    *   The comparison with training individual TFT models reinforces the benefits of iSTFT.
    *   Thorough mathematical explanation of the masking strategy.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The modifications to the underlying transformer architecture are relatively minor. The core contribution lies in the data preprocessing and the specialized attention masking.
    *   While the paper demonstrates the interpretability of iSTFT, a more in-depth analysis of the attention weight matrices, perhaps using visualization techniques or further quantitative metrics, could strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper could benefit from a comparison to other existing multi-output prediction methods specifically designed for dynamical systems, if they exist.  This would better contextualize the iSTFT's performance relative to alternatives.
    *   While three examples are provided, the scope of the examples could be extended.

*   **Potential Influence:** The iSTFT has the potential to influence the field by providing a more accurate and interpretable surrogate modeling technique for complex dynamical systems. The code/implementation will also contribute to how researchers and engineers can exploit the spatial-temporal correlation of dynamical systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of surrogate modeling and machine learning for dynamical systems. The iSTFT addresses a practical problem (predicting multiple outputs) with a relatively novel approach. The strengths of the paper, particularly the interpretable aspects and the demonstrated accuracy, are significant. While the core architecture of the transformer is not radically altered and could be strengthened with a more comprehensive comparison and deeper interpretability analysis, the specific application, attention mask design, and results make this a valuable and relevant contribution to the field, justifying a score of 7.5.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### JointDiT: Enhancing RGB-Depth Joint Modeling with Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00482v1)
- **Authors**: Kwon Byung-Ki, Qi Dai, Lee Hyoseok, Chong Luo, Tae-Hyun Oh
- **Abstract**: We present JointDiT, a diffusion transformer that models the joint distribution of RGB and depth. By leveraging the architectural benefit and outstanding image prior of the state-of-the-art diffusion transformer, JointDiT not only generates high-fidelity images but also produces geometrically plausible and accurate depth maps. This solid joint distribution modeling is achieved through two simple yet effective techniques that we propose, i.e., adaptive scheduling weights, which depend on the noise levels of each modality, and the unbalanced timestep sampling strategy. With these techniques, we train our model across all noise levels for each modality, enabling JointDiT to naturally handle various combinatorial generation tasks, including joint generation, depth estimation, and depth-conditioned image generation by simply controlling the timestep of each branch. JointDiT demonstrates outstanding joint generation performance. Furthermore, it achieves comparable results in depth estimation and depth-conditioned image generation, suggesting that joint distribution modeling can serve as a replaceable alternative to conditional generation. The project page is available at https://byungki-k.github.io/JointDiT/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the JointDiT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JointDiT, a diffusion transformer designed to model the joint distribution of RGB images and depth maps.  The core idea is to leverage the power of diffusion transformers, specifically the Flux architecture, to generate high-fidelity images and geometrically plausible depth maps simultaneously. JointDiT uses two key techniques: adaptive scheduling weights (adjusting the importance of each modality based on its noise level) and an unbalanced timestep sampling strategy (training on separate noise levels for each modality). This allows the model to perform various tasks like joint generation, depth estimation, and depth-conditioned image generation simply by adjusting the timestep of each branch. The authors demonstrate superior joint generation compared to prior work (LDM3D, JointNet) and comparable performance on depth estimation and depth-conditioned generation relative to specialized models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of diffusion transformers for joint RGB-Depth modeling has some novelty.  Previous approaches used U-Net based architectures which have inherent limitations.  The adaptive scheduling weights and unbalanced timestep sampling strategy are simpler innovations, but contribute significantly to the performance improvement. The idea of constructing a joint distribution usable for both generation and estimation in a single framework is well-motivated. However, the core components (diffusion transformers, LoRA, and cross-attention) are already established. The main novelty lies in the specific combination and tuning for this task.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it demonstrates the potential of joint distribution modeling as a unified approach for image and depth processing. This can simplify workflows and potentially lead to more robust systems, by allowing the model to learn interdependencies and perform various tasks within a single framework, improving generalizability.  The strong image prior and architectural benefits of diffusion transformers for dense prediction tasks are further solidified. The improved 3D lifting results and sharper depth maps compared to previous joint generation methods are a tangible contribution. While it's a practical demonstration of existing technologies, it is significant in that it enables more realistic image and depth map generation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents solid results demonstrating JointDiT's ability to generate high-quality images and depth maps. The 3D lifting visualisations provide a clear illustration of the geometric improvements.
    *   **Unified framework:** Successfully demonstrates joint modelling as an alternative to conditional modelling, which is versatile in various use cases.
    *   **Clear explanations:**  The paper clearly articulates the method and its benefits. The supplementary material provides a reasonable level of detail for reproducibility.
    *   **Well-motivated techniques:** The proposed adaptive scheduling weights and the unbalanced timestep sampling are well-motivated and simple to implement, yet effective.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Innovation:** While the results are strong, the technical novelty is somewhat incremental. JointDiT leverages existing technologies (diffusion transformers, LoRA, cross-attention) rather than introducing fundamentally new concepts.
    *   **Computational cost:** The higher computational cost for both training and inference (especially sampling time) compared to alternative methods is a limitation.
    *   **Limited scope:** The focus is primarily on RGB-Depth, and the generalization to other modalities is not extensively explored.
    *   **Reliance on Depth-Anything:** The reliance on Depth-Anything-V2 for depth maps and depth-conditioned generation means the overall system is dependent on its performance and potential biases.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the development of future joint modeling techniques for multi-modal data. It provides a blueprint for leveraging powerful diffusion transformers for joint image and depth processing, potentially inspiring further research in this area. It may motivate researchers to explore the proposed training techniques in the context of other modalities.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** JointDiT presents a useful contribution to the field of joint RGB-Depth modeling by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion transformers and the proposed training techniques. However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the heavy reliance on existing components. The strong empirical results and the demonstration of a unified framework warrant a score of 6.5, reflecting a valuable but not groundbreaking contribution. The potential influence of JointDiT is moderate, likely to inspire further exploration of similar techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### HalluMix: A Task-Agnostic, Multi-Domain Benchmark for Real-World Hallucination Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00506v1)
- **Authors**: Deanna Emery, Michael Goitia, Freddie Vargus, Iulia Neagu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains, detecting hallucinated content$\unicode{x2013}$text that is not grounded in supporting evidence$\unicode{x2013}$has become a critical challenge. Existing benchmarks for hallucination detection are often synthetically generated, narrowly focused on extractive question answering, and fail to capture the complexity of real-world scenarios involving multi-document contexts and full-sentence outputs. We introduce the HalluMix Benchmark, a diverse, task-agnostic dataset that includes examples from a range of domains and formats. Using this benchmark, we evaluate seven hallucination detection systems$\unicode{x2013}$both open and closed source$\unicode{x2013}$highlighting differences in performance across tasks, document lengths, and input representations. Our analysis highlights substantial performance disparities between short and long contexts, with critical implications for real-world Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) implementations. Quotient Detections achieves the best overall performance, with an accuracy of 0.82 and an F1 score of 0.84.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HalluMix, a new benchmark dataset designed for evaluating hallucination detection in large language models (LLMs). The dataset is task-agnostic and multi-domain, aiming to address the limitations of existing hallucination detection benchmarks that are often synthetically generated, focused on extractive question answering, and fail to capture the complexity of real-world scenarios with multi-document contexts and full-sentence outputs. The paper also evaluates seven state-of-the-art hallucination detection systems using HalluMix, highlighting performance differences across various tasks, document lengths, and input representations. The analysis reveals performance disparities between short and long contexts, with implications for Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) implementations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the *construction of a realistic, diverse benchmark*. Existing hallucination datasets often suffer from being too task-specific or relying on synthetic data. HalluMix aims to bridge this gap by incorporating data from various tasks (summarization, NLI, QA), domains (healthcare, law, science, news), and real-world scenarios (multi-document contexts, full-sentence outputs). While the individual data sources might not be novel, the careful curation, transformation, and combination of these sources into a unified benchmark is a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a *critical and timely problem*: the detection of hallucinations in LLMs, particularly in high-stakes domains. The HalluMix benchmark provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners to evaluate and compare different hallucination detection systems in a more realistic setting. The systematic evaluation of existing systems provides insights into their strengths, weaknesses, and suitability for different applications. The findings regarding the performance disparities between short and long contexts are particularly relevant for RAG implementations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Diversity:**  The dataset's diversity across tasks, domains, and formats is a major strength.
    *   **Realism:** The dataset is constructed from human-curated sources and aims to simulate real-world scenarios with multi-document contexts and full-sentence outputs.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of existing hallucination detection systems, highlighting their performance differences across various dimensions.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors make the dataset publicly available, promoting further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Transformation complexity:** While the variety of tasks is welcome, the number of applied transformations may obscure the origin dataset's specifics.
    *   **Limited scope of evaluated methods:** While the selected methods represent the state-of-the-art, the evaluation could be expanded to include a wider range of approaches.
    *   **Potential dataset bias:** The dataset might still contain biases from the original data sources or the transformation process.
    *   **Performance score variance:**  The paper mentions a high variance of performance scores on subdatasets. This suggests that results should be carefully interpreted, and that no detector works robustly across all types of data.

*   **Potential Influence:** The HalluMix benchmark has the potential to become a widely used resource in the field of hallucination detection. The paper's findings could influence the development of new and more robust hallucination detection systems, as well as the design of more effective RAG implementations.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a realistic and diverse benchmark for hallucination detection in LLMs. The systematic evaluation of existing systems provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses. While there are some limitations, such as the limited scope of evaluated methods and the potential for dataset bias, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The HalluMix benchmark has the potential to become a widely used resource in the field, promoting further research and development of more robust hallucination detection systems. The score of 8 reflects the paper's substantial contribution and potential influence, while acknowledging its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Self-Ablating Transformers: More Interpretability, Less Sparsity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00509v1)
- **Authors**: Jeremias Ferrao, Luhan Mikaelson, Keenan Pepper, Natalia Perez-Campanero Antolin
- **Abstract**: A growing intuition in machine learning suggests a link between sparsity and interpretability. We introduce a novel self-ablation mechanism to investigate this connection ante-hoc in the context of language transformers. Our approach dynamically enforces a k-winner-takes-all constraint, forcing the model to demonstrate selective activation across neuron and attention units. Unlike post-hoc methods that analyze already-trained models, our approach integrates interpretability directly into model training, promoting feature localization from inception. Training small models on the TinyStories dataset and employing interpretability tests, we find that self-ablation leads to more localized circuits, concentrated feature representations, and increased neuron specialization without compromising language modelling performance. Surprisingly, our method also decreased overall sparsity, indicating that self-ablation promotes specialization rather than widespread inactivity. This reveals a complex interplay between sparsity and interpretability, where decreased global sparsity can coexist with increased local specialization, leading to enhanced interpretability. To facilitate reproducibility, we make our code available at https://github.com/keenanpepper/self-ablating-transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel "self-ablation" mechanism for training language transformers that encourages interpretability by dynamically enforcing a k-winner-takes-all (kWTA) constraint on neuron and attention unit activations. This approach trains models to exhibit more selective activation patterns, essentially pruning less relevant connections during training, without modifying the model architecture at inference time.  The authors compare models trained with self-ablation to a baseline transformer on the TinyStories dataset, using metrics such as Automatic Circuit Discovery (ACDC), Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), neuron explainability, and neuron graph analysis to evaluate interpretability. They find that self-ablation leads to more localized circuits, concentrated feature representations, and increased neuron specialization.  Surprisingly, they also observe a *decrease* in overall sparsity, suggesting that focused specialization, rather than simply reducing the number of active components, is key to enhanced interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novelty:** The self-ablation mechanism is a creative approach to directly integrate interpretability considerations into the training process, rather than relying on post-hoc analyses.  The dynamic application of kWTA through learned gating weights, and its removal during inference, is a strong design choice. The finding that increased interpretability can coexist with *decreased* global sparsity is a significant and counter-intuitive result.
    * **Methodology:** The paper uses a comprehensive suite of interpretability metrics, providing a multifaceted view of the impact of self-ablation. The comparisons with a baseline transformer are well-controlled. The code availability is a definite plus for reproducibility.  The exploration of both "local" and "global" ablation strategies adds depth to the analysis.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand. The methodology is explained clearly, and the results are presented in a logical manner.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Dataset Limitations:**  The TinyStories dataset, while useful for experimentation, severely limits the complexity of the learned circuits and the generalizability of the findings.  Complex language phenomena are absent, making it unclear whether the benefits of self-ablation will extend to more realistic scenarios. The paper even acknowledges this limitation.
    * **Model Size:** The experiments are conducted on relatively small models (3.6M parameters). It's uncertain how well the self-ablation mechanism will scale to larger architectures and more complex tasks. Will memory or computation become limiting factors?
    * **Perplexity Trade-off:** While the increase in perplexity is characterized as "modest", it's still a notable cost.  A rigorous investigation into how to mitigate this performance degradation would strengthen the paper.  Are there hyperparameter settings or alternative ablation strategies that could minimize the perplexity increase without sacrificing interpretability?
    * **Interpretability Metrics:** While the paper uses a variety of metrics, some of these (e.g., the automated neuron explainability scores) are themselves based on large language models and are subject to their own biases and limitations. Are they truly objective measures of interpretability? A stronger argument for the validity and reliability of these metrics could be made.
    * **Lack of ablation of hyperparameters of proposed approach**: There isn't a systematic ablation of hyperparameters for kWTA or ablation strength. What is the effect on interpretability and performance?

* **Significance:**

    The paper challenges the prevailing assumption that sparsity is directly correlated with interpretability, demonstrating that a more nuanced approach focused on feature specialization can be more effective. The self-ablation mechanism provides a concrete method for designing more interpretable models, potentially paving the way for new architectural innovations. The work encourages a shift in focus from simply minimizing the number of active components to promoting more organized and specialized representations.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its strengths, the paper's reliance on the TinyStories dataset and small models significantly limits the impact and generalizability of the findings. The perplexity trade-off, while acknowledged, requires further investigation.  While the concept of self-ablation is innovative, its ultimate significance hinges on its ability to scale to larger models and more complex tasks without substantial performance degradation.  The contribution of this paper is significant because it introduces a new concept and empirically shows it's promising, but because of its significant limitations I feel like a lower score would be the most accurate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Safety-Critical Traffic Simulation with Guided Latent Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00515v1)
- **Authors**: Mingxing Peng, Ruoyu Yao, Xusen Guo, Yuting Xie, Xianda Chen, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Safety-critical traffic simulation plays a crucial role in evaluating autonomous driving systems under rare and challenging scenarios. However, existing approaches often generate unrealistic scenarios due to insufficient consideration of physical plausibility and suffer from low generation efficiency. To address these limitations, we propose a guided latent diffusion model (LDM) capable of generating physically realistic and adversarial safety-critical traffic scenarios. Specifically, our model employs a graph-based variational autoencoder (VAE) to learn a compact latent space that captures complex multi-agent interactions while improving computational efficiency. Within this latent space, the diffusion model performs the denoising process to produce realistic trajectories. To enable controllable and adversarial scenario generation, we introduce novel guidance objectives that drive the diffusion process toward producing adversarial and behaviorally realistic driving behaviors. Furthermore, we develop a sample selection module based on physical feasibility checks to further enhance the physical plausibility of the generated scenarios. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that our method achieves superior adversarial effectiveness and generation efficiency compared to existing baselines while maintaining a high level of realism. Our work provides an effective tool for realistic safety-critical scenario simulation, paving the way for more robust evaluation of autonomous driving systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a guided Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) for safety-critical traffic simulation. The goal is to generate realistic and adversarial traffic scenarios to rigorously evaluate autonomous driving systems. The model uses a graph-based variational autoencoder (VAE) to learn a compact latent space representing multi-agent interactions.  A diffusion model then operates within this latent space to generate trajectories. The authors introduce novel guidance objectives and a physical feasibility check to enable controllable and adversarial scenario generation. Experimental results on the nuScenes dataset show that the method outperforms existing baselines in terms of adversarial effectiveness, generation efficiency, and realism.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits a good level of novelty by combining several recent advances into a coherent framework. The use of an LDM for traffic simulation is not entirely new, but the specific combination of a graph-based VAE for latent space representation *coupled with* novel guidance objectives to drive adversarial scenario generation *and* a physical feasibility check for ensuring plausibility is a significant contribution. The guidance objectives, in particular, seem to be well-designed for promoting safety-critical situations. The introduction of a separate sample selection module based on physics checks further adds to the contribution. This differentiated it from basic adoption of latent diffusion models in safety-critical simulations.

*   **Significance:**  Safety-critical traffic simulation is a crucial area for ensuring the reliability of autonomous vehicles. By improving the realism, efficiency, and adversarial effectiveness of traffic scenario generation, this work has the potential to make a significant impact on the field. The results indicate that the model is capable of generating more challenging scenarios for AVs compared to existing methods, which will help to expose potential vulnerabilities and enhance robustness. A key element of the significance is improvement in efficiency compared to optimization-based methods like AdvSim and Strive.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The model combines established techniques (LDM, GNNs, VAEs) in a novel way to address a specific problem.
    *   The guidance objectives appear to be well-designed to induce adversarial behaviors.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over baselines. The metrics are comprehensive, covering adversariality, realism, diversity, and efficiency.
    *   The paper provides a clear and well-written description of the model and experiments.
    *   The ablation study offers valuable insights into the contribution of the guidance mechanism.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper mentions a rule-based planner for the ego vehicle. Using such a rudimentary planner might limit the scope of safety validation. A more sophisticated learned planner might be more representative of real-world AVs.
    *   The reliance on the nuScenes dataset limits the evaluation to a specific type of urban environment. Generalization to other environments should be considered.
    *   The physical feasibility check seems relatively simple (based on longitudinal and lateral acceleration limits). The robustness of scenarios could be improved with a deeper consideration of physical constraints of the vehicles and the environment.
    *   The paper mentions fixing an adversarial agent. In real complex and safety-critical scenarios agents' goals may shift as the state of the world evolves. While this decision could have been motivated by comparisons to the existing baselines or for easier controllability, it may limit the model's ability to represent real-world behavior.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence the development of more robust and efficient safety validation tools for autonomous vehicles. The combination of LDM and guidance objectives can inspire new approaches to generating challenging and realistic traffic scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper builds upon existing techniques, it introduces a novel and well-integrated approach to address a crucial problem in autonomous driving safety. The combination of a latent diffusion model with guidance objectives and physical feasibility checks leads to significant improvements in adversarial effectiveness, efficiency, and realism compared to existing methods. While there are some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the paper has the potential to make a meaningful impact on the field. This research goes beyond just using diffusion models. With its physics-based model and adversarial guidance, it offers improvements that are significant for autonomous driving.

### 100 Days After DeepSeek-R1: A Survey on Replication Studies and More Directions for Reasoning Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00551v1)
- **Authors**: Chong Zhang, Yue Deng, Xiang Lin, Bin Wang, Dianwen Ng, Hai Ye, Xingxuan Li, Yao Xiao, Zhanfeng Mo, Qi Zhang, Lidong Bing
- **Abstract**: The recent development of reasoning language models (RLMs) represents a novel evolution in large language models. In particular, the recent release of DeepSeek-R1 has generated widespread social impact and sparked enthusiasm in the research community for exploring the explicit reasoning paradigm of language models. However, the implementation details of the released models have not been fully open-sourced by DeepSeek, including DeepSeek-R1-Zero, DeepSeek-R1, and the distilled small models. As a result, many replication studies have emerged aiming to reproduce the strong performance achieved by DeepSeek-R1, reaching comparable performance through similar training procedures and fully open-source data resources. These works have investigated feasible strategies for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), focusing on data preparation and method design, yielding various valuable insights. In this report, we provide a summary of recent replication studies to inspire future research. We primarily focus on SFT and RLVR as two main directions, introducing the details for data construction, method design and training procedure of current replication studies. Moreover, we conclude key findings from the implementation details and experimental results reported by these studies, anticipating to inspire future research. We also discuss additional techniques of enhancing RLMs, highlighting the potential of expanding the application scope of these models, and discussing the challenges in development. By this survey, we aim to help researchers and developers of RLMs stay updated with the latest advancements, and seek to inspire new ideas to further enhance RLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper description:

**Summary:**

The paper is a survey of recent replication studies and further directions in the field of reasoning language models (RLMs), particularly following the release of DeepSeek-R1. It focuses on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) as the two main training paradigms used in these replication attempts. The survey discusses data construction, method design, training procedures, and key findings from these replication studies.  It also explores additional techniques for enhancing RLMs, such as reward modeling and preference optimization, and discusses challenges and future directions, including generalizability, safety, and multimodality. The paper aims to help researchers and developers stay updated with advancements in RLMs and inspire new ideas in the field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive collation and synthesis of the numerous replication attempts of DeepSeek-R1.  While the individual techniques (SFT, RLVR, PPO, GRPO etc.) are not new *per se*, the survey's value is in mapping the landscape of open-source efforts to reproduce a state-of-the-art model. The discussion of challenges and future directions also adds some value, though these are largely speculative. A weakness is that, given the rapid pace of development in LLMs, the snapshot provided by the survey may quickly become outdated. The inclusion of additional techniques, such as preference optimization, and discussion of broader topics like safety and generalizability adds value beyond a simple enumeration of replication attempts.

*   **Significance:**  The paper has significance within the RLM research community. DeepSeek-R1 generated substantial interest, and the replication studies are important for understanding the key ingredients for success. By summarizing these efforts, the survey can accelerate future research by providing a centralized resource for researchers and practitioners. It aids in understanding data needs, algorithmic choices and practical considerations for achieving high performance in RLMs. It serves as a valuable starting point for researchers who want to enter this field or improve existing models. It discusses significant considerations like generalizability and potential trade-offs with SFT and RL training which are essential for real-world deployment of RLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive coverage of DeepSeek-R1 replication efforts.
    *   Clear organization by training methodology (SFT, RLVR).
    *   Detailed analysis of data curation pipelines and datasets.
    *   Discussion of algorithmic variations and their motivations.
    *   Identification of key challenges and future directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The field of RLMs is evolving very rapidly, therefore some of the content might soon be outdated.
    *   The analysis is primarily descriptive, lacks strong theoretical contributions.
    *   The future directions are relatively high-level and could benefit from more concrete suggestions.
    *   Relatively little discussion on the trade-offs of safety implementations and reasoning abilities.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper is a valuable contribution to the field, particularly given the open-source focus of the RLM landscape. It effectively maps the "state of play" regarding the reproduction of DeepSeek-R1 and provides a good starting point for future research. It highlights the significance of data and the challenges associated with it in RLMs. However, it stops short of delivering truly *novel* insights or theoretical frameworks. The paper's value is primarily in organizing, synthesizing, and presenting information, rather than producing significantly novel results. Thus, it deserves a respectable, but not exceptional score.

### Triggering Hallucinations in LLMs: A Quantitative Study of Prompt-Induced Hallucination in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00557v1)
- **Authors**: Makoto Sato
- **Abstract**: Hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) present a growing challenge across real-world applications, from healthcare to law, where factual reliability is essential. Despite advances in alignment and instruction tuning, LLMs can still generate outputs that are fluent yet fundamentally untrue. Understanding the cognitive dynamics that underlie these hallucinations remains an open problem. In this study, we propose a prompt-based framework to systematically trigger and quantify hallucination: a Hallucination-Inducing Prompt (HIP), which synthetically fuses semantically distant concepts (e.g., periodic table of elements and tarot divination) in a misleading way, and a Hallucination Quantifying Prompt (HQP), which scores the plausibility, confidence, and coherence of the output. Controlled experiments across multiple LLMs revealed that HIPs consistently produced less coherent and more hallucinated responses than their null-fusion controls. These effects varied across models, with reasoning-oriented LLMs showing distinct profiles from general-purpose ones. Our framework provides a reproducible testbed for studying hallucination vulnerability, and opens the door to developing safer, more introspective LLMs that can detect and self-regulate the onset of conceptual instability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Triggering Hallucinations in LLMs: A Quantitative Study of Prompt-Induced Hallucination in Large Language Models" investigates how specific prompt structures can reliably induce hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces the Hallucination-Inducing Prompt (HIP), designed to synthetically fuse semantically distant concepts in a misleading manner, and the Hallucination Quantifying Prompt (HQP), used to evaluate the plausibility, confidence, and coherence of the LLM's output. Through controlled experiments across various LLMs, the study demonstrates that HIPs consistently generate less coherent and more hallucinated responses compared to control prompts. The research also reveals model-specific differences in hallucination susceptibility and suggests that semantic fusion without conceptual grounding is a key risk factor for hallucinations. The authors propose their framework as a reproducible testbed for studying hallucination vulnerability and for developing safer, more introspective LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Prompt Engineering Approach:** The HIP-HQP framework provides a systematic and reproducible method for inducing and quantifying hallucinations. This is a significant contribution, as it moves beyond treating hallucinations as random errors and towards understanding them as potentially inducible cognitive responses. The prompts are short, focused and effective.
*   **Systematic Experimentation:** The study employs a controlled experimental design, comparing HIPs with null-fusion controls across a diverse range of LLMs. This allows for a robust analysis of model-specific hallucination profiles and the effects of semantic fusion. The disposable session method is also a good way of minimizing memory effects.
*   **Identification of Key Risk Factor:** The finding that semantic fusion without conceptual grounding is a major driver of hallucinations is a valuable insight. This highlights the importance of conceptual grounding in LLM training and prompt design. The results regarding reasoning-oriented vs general-purpose LLMs are also interesting and warrant further investigation.
*   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is clearly written, well-structured, and provides sufficient details about the experimental methodology. The inclusion of representative PIH logs in the appendix is helpful.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on LLMs for HQP Evaluation:** The study relies on GPT-3 for HQP evaluation, raising concerns about objectivity and potential biases. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, it's a significant factor in the validity of the quantification. Using multiple LLMs as HQP for cross validation would increase the robustness of the analysis.
*   **Limited Scope of Semantic Fusion:** While the study focuses on semantic fusion of "periodic table and tarot", it would be beneficial to explore a wider range of semantically distant concepts to determine the generalizability of the findings. The study should clarify the role of human bias in designing the HIPs.
*   **Lack of Internal Signal Analysis:** The study primarily relies on external behavioral measurements through HQP evaluations. Incorporating internal signal analysis, such as logit distributions and attention patterns, could provide a more nuanced understanding of the underlying mechanisms of prompt-induced hallucinations.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper is definitely more than a small step forward in the right direction. The focus on prompt structure as a systematic way to trigger hallucinations is innovative and has the potential to significantly impact how we test and improve LLM safety and reliability. The research provides a new tool for investigating the boundaries of LLM semantic understanding and coherence. However, the dependence on an LLM for evaluation (HQP) weakens the objectivity of the overall approach, limiting the wider impact.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper provides a novel and systematic approach to understanding and inducing hallucinations in LLMs. The HIP-HQP framework is a valuable tool for research and development in this area. The finding that semantic fusion without conceptual grounding is a key risk factor is significant and points to important areas for future research and development. However, the study's reliance on LLMs for evaluation is a notable limitation, as well as the limited range of tested concepts. Because of these weaknesses, a higher score isn't justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### X-ray illicit object detection using hybrid CNN-transformer neural network architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00564v1)
- **Authors**: Jorgen Cani, Christos Diou, Spyridon Evangelatos, Panagiotis Radoglou-Grammatikis, Vasileios Argyriou, Panagiotis Sarigiannidis, Iraklis Varlamis, Georgios Th. Papadopoulos
- **Abstract**: In the field of X-ray security applications, even the smallest details can significantly impact outcomes. Objects that are heavily occluded or intentionally concealed pose a great challenge for detection, whether by human observation or through advanced technological applications. While certain Deep Learning (DL) architectures demonstrate strong performance in processing local information, such as Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), others excel in handling distant information, e.g., transformers. In X-ray security imaging the literature has been dominated by the use of CNN-based methods, while the integration of the two aforementioned leading architectures has not been sufficiently explored. In this paper, various hybrid CNN-transformer architectures are evaluated against a common CNN object detection baseline, namely YOLOv8. In particular, a CNN (HGNetV2) and a hybrid CNN-transformer (Next-ViT-S) backbone are combined with different CNN/transformer detection heads (YOLOv8 and RT-DETR). The resulting architectures are comparatively evaluated on three challenging public X-ray inspection datasets, namely EDS, HiXray, and PIDray. Interestingly, while the YOLOv8 detector with its default backbone (CSP-DarkNet53) is generally shown to be advantageous on the HiXray and PIDray datasets, when a domain distribution shift is incorporated in the X-ray images (as happens in the EDS datasets), hybrid CNN-transformer architectures exhibit increased robustness. Detailed comparative evaluation results, including object-level detection performance and object-size error analysis, demonstrate the strengths and weaknesses of each architectural combination and suggest guidelines for future research. The source code and network weights of the models employed in this study are available at https://github.com/jgenc/xray-comparative-evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of hybrid CNN-transformer architectures for illicit object detection in X-ray security imaging. It compares various configurations, combining CNN (HGNetV2) and hybrid CNN-transformer (Next-ViT-S) backbones with CNN/transformer detection heads (YOLOv8 and RT-DETR), against a standard CNN baseline (YOLOv8 with CSP-DarkNet53) on three public X-ray datasets (EDS, HiXray, and PIDray). The key finding is that while the CNN baseline performs well on HiXray and PIDray, the hybrid architectures demonstrate increased robustness on the EDS dataset, which incorporates a domain shift due to the use of multiple X-ray scanners. The paper provides a comparative evaluation and suggests guidelines for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem in X-ray security imaging.
    *   It systematically explores different hybrid CNN-transformer architectures, which is a relatively underexplored area in the field of X-ray imaging.
    *   The use of multiple datasets allows for a more comprehensive evaluation of the models' performance and robustness, particularly the inclusion of the EDS dataset which introduces a domain shift.
    *   The paper presents a detailed comparative analysis, including object-level performance and object-size error analysis.
    *   The authors make their code and models publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper shows increased robustness of hybrid models on the EDS dataset, the CNN baseline still achieves competitive performance on HiXray and PIDray. This suggests the benefits of hybrid architectures are conditional and not universally superior.
    *   The paper doesn't delve deeply into the reasons *why* hybrid architectures are more robust to domain shift in the EDS dataset. Hypotheses are presented (e.g., increased global context), but further investigation (e.g., visualization of attention maps, feature analysis) would strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper could benefit from exploring alternative training strategies such as fine-tuning of pretrained models or domain adaptation techniques to mitigate the distribution shift in the EDS dataset.
    *   The gains in mAP on EDS are relatively small which brings into question the practical value or need to switch to hybrid architectures.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper has a moderate level of novelty. While hybrid CNN-transformer architectures are becoming more common in computer vision, their systematic evaluation in the context of X-ray security imaging is a contribution. The identification of increased robustness in the presence of domain shift (as seen in the EDS dataset) is a potentially significant finding, though it requires further investigation to fully understand the underlying mechanisms and generalize its impact. The paper fills a gap by showing that the field can benefit from the introduction of hybrid CNN-Transformer architectures.
    The paper introduces hybrid architectures into the area, but the findings may not be a true breakthrough, but the thorough evaluation is helpful for researchers to know about the strengths and weaknesses of the approaches.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    I assign a score of **6** due to:

    *   The systematic exploration of hybrid architectures in a specific and important domain (X-ray security).
    *   The valuable finding of increased robustness to domain shift, though this warrants further investigation.
    *   The availability of code and models for reproducibility.
    *   The limited magnitude of the improvements and lack of deep understanding of the benefits of the hybrid architecture. The paper identifies but doesn't fully explain the reason for these improvements.

### FreqKV: Frequency Domain Key-Value Compression for Efficient Context Window Extension
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00570v1)
- **Authors**: Jushi Kai, Boyi Zeng, Yixuan Wang, Haoli Bai, Bo Jiang, Zhouhan Lin
- **Abstract**: Extending the context window in large language models (LLMs) is essential for applications involving long-form content generation. However, the linear increase in key-value (KV) cache memory requirements and the quadratic complexity of self-attention with respect to sequence length present significant challenges during fine-tuning and inference. Existing methods suffer from performance degradation when extending to longer contexts. In this work, we introduce a novel context extension method that optimizes both fine-tuning and inference efficiency. Our method exploits a key observation: in the frequency domain, the energy distribution of the KV cache is primarily concentrated in low-frequency components. By filtering out the high-frequency components, the KV cache can be effectively compressed with minimal information loss. Building on this insight, we propose an efficient compression technique, FreqKV, that iteratively compresses the increasing KV cache to a fixed size in the frequency domain, applicable to both fine-tuning and inference. FreqKV introduces no additional parameters or architectural modifications. With minimal fine-tuning, LLMs can learn to leverage the limited cache that is compressed in the frequency domain and extend the context window efficiently. Experiments on various long context language modeling and understanding tasks demonstrate the efficiency and efficacy of the proposed method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FreqKV: Frequency Domain Key-Value Compression for Efficient Context Window Extension":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FreqKV, a novel method for extending the context window of Large Language Models (LLMs) by compressing the key-value (KV) cache in the frequency domain.  It's based on the observation that the energy distribution of the KV cache is concentrated in low-frequency components. FreqKV filters out high-frequency components, thereby compressing the cache with minimal information loss. This compression is done iteratively as the KV cache grows, triggered when the cache reaches a certain size, and involves Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) and Inverse DCT (IDCT). The method is applicable to both fine-tuning and inference, requires minimal fine-tuning, and introduces no new parameters or architectural changes.  Experimental results on long-context language modeling and understanding tasks demonstrate the efficiency and effectiveness of FreqKV.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of compressing the KV cache in the frequency domain is relatively novel in the context of LLM context extension. Prior methods focused on eviction, merging, or adding compression modules which differ from FreqKV's approach. While the use of frequency domain techniques is established in image compression and has been explored in Transformers (e.g., FNet), its application to compressing KV caches in decoder-only LLMs is a non-trivial adaptation. The iterative compression strategy and the attention sink handling are also positive additions. However, the idea of exploiting frequency domain properties for compression in other areas exists, slightly lowering the score.

*   **Significance:** Extending the context window of LLMs is a significant area of research. The linear increase in memory and quadratic increase in computation costs with context length presents a major bottleneck. FreqKV addresses this bottleneck effectively and efficiently. The results demonstrate comparable or even superior performance (in some cases) to methods that use a full KV cache or additional compressors. The method is also practical, requiring minimal fine-tuning and no architectural changes. This ease of integration is crucial for widespread adoption. The performance improvement compared to the baselines across multiple tasks is also a positive sign. The fact that this technique focuses on decoder-only architectures further adds to its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** FreqKV introduces no new parameters or architectural changes, making it easy to implement and integrate into existing LLMs.
    *   **Efficiency:**  The compression is performed on-the-fly, with manageable overhead. The iterative compression means older tokens are more compressed making it efficient for long contexts.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results show good performance on both language modeling and understanding tasks, surpassing some existing methods, especially in the Needle-in-a-Haystack test.
    *   **Decoder-only focused:** Specifically addresses decoder-only architectures popular for generation.
    *   **Attention Sinks:**  The method specifically addresses and leverages insights regarding attention sinks in LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited theoretical analysis:**  While the paper provides empirical evidence for the effectiveness of FreqKV, a deeper theoretical analysis of why this frequency-based compression works so well for KV caches would be beneficial. Understanding the information content of different frequency components in KV caches is crucial and could benefit from a more formal information-theoretic perspective.
    *   **Parameter sensitivity:**  The paper mentions the retaining ratio 'y'. It would benefit from a more in-depth sensitivity analysis of this parameter and guidance on how to choose it optimally for different tasks and model sizes.
    *   **Limited model scale analysis:**  The paper focuses primarily on LLaMA-2-7b and LLaMA-3-8b. Assessing the effectiveness of FreqKV on larger models would strengthen the contribution further. The paper also could have had a wider variety of LLM architectures.
    *   **RoPE interaction degradation:** As demonstrated in the appendix, the interaction between RoPE and the frequency-based compression shows a negative impact.  More work may be needed for more specific RoPE optimizations.

*   **Potential Influence:**  FreqKV has the potential to influence the field by providing a simple and effective way to extend the context window of LLMs without significant performance degradation. The ease of implementation could lead to its adoption in various applications, and the frequency domain perspective may inspire new methods for compression and optimization in LLMs.

*The comparison between Dropping and FreqKV also highlights the importance of retaining earlier information, demonstrating why compressing instead of simply dropping information is better for long context tasks.*

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**  FreqKV is a solid contribution with a novel approach and practical benefits. Its simplicity, efficiency, and competitive performance make it a valuable addition to the field. However, the lack of deeper theoretical analysis, limited parameter sensitivity analysis, model scale analysis, and sub-optimal interaction with RoPE hold it back from a higher score. The impact could be significant, but further research is needed to fully understand its potential and address its limitations. The weaknesses mentioned above could inform future research directions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Block Circulant Adapter for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00582v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyu Ding, Meiqi Wang, Siyu Liao, Zhongfeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) is difficult due to their huge model size. Recent Fourier domain-based methods show potential for reducing fine-tuning costs. We propose a block circulant matrix-based fine-tuning method with a stable training heuristic to leverage the properties of circulant matrices and one-dimensional Fourier transforms to reduce storage and computation costs. Experiments show that our method uses $14\times$ less number of parameters than VeRA, $16\times$ smaller than LoRA and $32\times$ less FLOPs than FourierFT, while maintaining close or better task performance. Our approach presents a promising way in frequency domain to fine-tune large models on downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Block Circulant Adapters (BCA), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs). It leverages block circulant matrices and FFT operations to reduce storage and computation costs. The authors address the potential gradient explosion risk associated with block circulant matrices by proposing a learning rate adjustment heuristic. Experimental results on GLUE benchmark datasets and Alpaca/GSM8K datasets demonstrate that BCA achieves comparable or better performance than existing methods like LoRA and FourierFT while using significantly fewer parameters and FLOPs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel adaptation of block circulant matrices, previously used in other areas of deep learning, to the specific problem of efficient fine-tuning of LLMs. While the use of circulant matrices and Fourier transforms for compression and speedup is not entirely new in deep learning, the application to LLM fine-tuning and the specific design of BCA, along with the proposed stabilization heuristic, can be considered novel.  The authors also provide a theoretical justification for a training heuristic, which is relatively uncommon.

**Significance:** The primary significance lies in the potential to reduce the computational burden of fine-tuning LLMs, making it more accessible and practical. Demonstrating comparable or better performance with significantly reduced parameters and FLOPs is a valuable contribution. The work also provides useful insights into the challenges of training with structured matrices in the context of LLMs, specifically, the gradient instability issue. Further, the experiments are conducted on standard benchmarks, making it easier to compare to other PEFT methods.

**Strengths:**
*   **Efficiency:** BCA demonstrates substantial reductions in parameter count and FLOPs compared to competing methods while maintaining competitive performance.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:** The paper includes a theoretical analysis of the gradient explosion risk and provides a corresponding heuristic to address it. The authors support their reasoning with a mathematical formalism and an empricial evaluation.
*   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on standard benchmarks validate the effectiveness of the approach.
*   **Convergence Heuristic:** It addresses an important practical issue in using circulant matrices in LLMs, namely, divergence.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the paper offers theoretical analysis, further investigation into why the heuristic works so well could enhance its impact. Also a more theoretical comparison to FFT could be helpful, as the authors adopt a learning rate adjustment heuristic.
*   **Scalability beyond parameter count:** The paper primarily focuses on reducing parameters and FLOPs, but more comprehensive analysis of practical aspects like training time, memory footprint, would strengthen the claims.
*   **Generalization:** While the experiments on the ViT model demonstrate the versatility of BCA,  further evaluation on a wider range of LLMs and downstream tasks would be beneficial. It is worth mentioning here that additional evaluations in Appendix A does provide a useful demonstration on vision transformer models.

**Overall Impact and Justification:**
The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of parameter-efficient fine-tuning. The combination of a structured matrix approach, a stabilization heuristic, and strong empirical results makes it a valuable addition.  It addresses a key challenge of LLM fine-tuning—reducing computational costs—and offers a practical solution. While the novelty isn't groundbreaking, the significance of the method's efficiency and stability, coupled with the theoretical insight, elevates its importance. It's a well-executed piece of engineering with solid validation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ParkDiffusion: Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Multi-Modal Trajectory Prediction for Automated Parking using Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00586v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarong Wei, Niclas Vödisch, Anna Rehr, Christian Feist, Abhinav Valada
- **Abstract**: Automated parking is a critical feature of Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS), where accurate trajectory prediction is essential to bridge perception and planning modules. Despite its significance, research in this domain remains relatively limited, with most existing studies concentrating on single-modal trajectory prediction of vehicles. In this work, we propose ParkDiffusion, a novel approach that predicts the trajectories of both vehicles and pedestrians in automated parking scenarios. ParkDiffusion employs diffusion models to capture the inherent uncertainty and multi-modality of future trajectories, incorporating several key innovations. First, we propose a dual map encoder that processes soft semantic cues and hard geometric constraints using a two-step cross-attention mechanism. Second, we introduce an adaptive agent type embedding module, which dynamically conditions the prediction process on the distinct characteristics of vehicles and pedestrians. Third, to ensure kinematic feasibility, our model outputs control signals that are subsequently used within a kinematic framework to generate physically feasible trajectories. We evaluate ParkDiffusion on the Dragon Lake Parking (DLP) dataset and the Intersections Drone (inD) dataset. Our work establishes a new baseline for heterogeneous trajectory prediction in parking scenarios, outperforming existing methods by a considerable margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ParkDiffusion: Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Multi-Modal Trajectory Prediction for Automated Parking using Diffusion Models" introduces a novel approach for trajectory prediction in automated parking scenarios.  It addresses the unique challenges of parking environments, such as less structured layouts, unpredictable user behavior, and the shared space between vehicles and vulnerable road users (VRUs). ParkDiffusion employs diffusion models and incorporates several key innovations: a dual map encoder to process soft semantic cues and hard geometric constraints, an adaptive agent type embedding module to dynamically condition predictions on vehicle and pedestrian characteristics, and a kinematic refinement module to generate physically feasible trajectories.  The model is evaluated on the Dragon Lake Parking (DLP) and Intersections Drone (inD) datasets, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of diffusion models to the challenging problem of heterogeneous trajectory prediction in parking environments.  While diffusion models for trajectory prediction are not entirely new, the combination of:

    *   A dual map encoder that distinctly handles *soft* (e.g., lane markings) and *hard* (e.g., parked cars) constraints, which is a clever way to encode context that might be more specific to automated parking compared to general driving.
    *   Agent type embedding, which is particularly important in the context of parking because VRUs like pedestrians have very different motion characteristics.
    *   Kinematic refinement to ensure physically plausible trajectories. This is quite important for the application.

    ... makes this a novel solution within trajectory prediction for parking scenarios. Furthermore, it's claimed that the paper is the first to explicitly consider VRUs (vehicles and pedestrians) in parking trajectory prediction, which increases safety.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important gap in the existing literature by focusing on the under-studied domain of trajectory prediction in automated parking. As automated parking systems become more prevalent, accurate and reliable trajectory prediction becomes critical for ensuring safety and efficiency. By explicitly modeling the behavior of both vehicles and pedestrians, the proposed approach contributes to safer and more robust automated parking systems. The demonstration of superior performance on standard benchmarks validates the effectiveness of the proposed innovations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Focus:**  The paper targets a relevant and under-explored problem in autonomous driving.
    *   **Technical Soundness:**  The proposed architecture is well-motivated and integrates several relevant techniques (diffusion models, attention mechanisms, kinematic refinement) in a meaningful way.
    *   **Experimental Validation:**  The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation on two datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach and providing a detailed ablation study to assess the impact of individual components.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Map Information:** The dual map encoder relies on accurate map information, which may not always be available or reliable in real-world parking environments. The robustness of the approach to noisy or incomplete map data could be further explored.
    *   **Incremental contribution:** While the paper claims it leverages a novel approach by implementing existing concepts into a diffusion framework, there is minimal discussion regarding the actual impact of implementing these in a parking context.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and safer automated parking systems. The proposed ParkDiffusion model can serve as a valuable baseline for future research in this area, and the insights gained from the ablation studies can guide the design of improved trajectory prediction algorithms.
    *   The use of diffusions models for this task has also been done a number of times already, suggesting less novelty.

*   **Overall assessment:** The contribution is significant and has been validated by a number of controlled experiments in a range of realistic settings. The approach also provides a significant improvement over several state-of-the-art algorithms in the same domain.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and valuable contribution to the field of trajectory prediction for automated parking. The application of diffusion models to this domain, along with the introduction of the dual map encoder, agent type embedding, and kinematic refinement module, represents a significant advance over existing methods. The thorough experimental evaluation and clear presentation further enhance the paper's quality. However, the dependency on map information and the need for further exploration of the model's robustness under real-world conditions, along with the incrementally novel approach relative to diffusion modeling for general trajectory prediction, temper the score.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can LLMs Help Improve Analogical Reasoning For Strategic Decisions? Experimental Evidence from Humans and GPT-4
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00603v1)
- **Authors**: Phanish Puranam, Prothit Sen, Maciej Workiewicz
- **Abstract**: This study investigates whether large language models, specifically GPT4, can match human capabilities in analogical reasoning within strategic decision making contexts. Using a novel experimental design involving source to target matching, we find that GPT4 achieves high recall by retrieving all plausible analogies but suffers from low precision, frequently applying incorrect analogies based on superficial similarities. In contrast, human participants exhibit high precision but low recall, selecting fewer analogies yet with stronger causal alignment. These findings advance theory by identifying matching, the evaluative phase of analogical reasoning, as a distinct step that requires accurate causal mapping beyond simple retrieval. While current LLMs are proficient in generating candidate analogies, humans maintain a comparative advantage in recognizing deep structural similarities across domains. Error analysis reveals that AI errors arise from surface level matching, whereas human errors stem from misinterpretations of causal structure. Taken together, the results suggest a productive division of labor in AI assisted organizational decision making where LLMs may serve as broad analogy generators, while humans act as critical evaluators, applying the most contextually appropriate analogies to strategic problems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the analogical reasoning abilities of GPT-4 compared to humans in strategic decision-making contexts. Using a novel experimental design with two source analogs and two target problems, the study finds that GPT-4 demonstrates high recall (retrieving nearly all relevant analogies) but low precision (frequently applying incorrect analogies based on superficial features). Humans, conversely, exhibit high precision but low recall, selecting fewer but more causally aligned analogies. The authors conclude that GPT-4 excels at analogy generation, while humans retain cognitive superiority in mapping causal structures. This suggests a potentially productive division of labor: AI as an analogy generator, and humans as critical evaluators of contextually relevant causal schemas. The paper emphasizes the importance of the 'matching' phase of analogical reasoning (evaluating potential analogies) and contributes by highlighting a key distinction in how AI and humans approach analogical reasoning, offering insights for integrating AI into strategic decision-making processes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This paper makes a valuable contribution by empirically investigating and directly comparing the analogical reasoning capabilities of humans and a state-of-the-art LLM (GPT-4) within a strategic decision-making context. The study addresses a significant gap in the literature, as much of the previous work focuses on more constrained tasks rather than the open-ended, linguistically framed problems that managers face. The experimental design is innovative, introducing a "matching problem" that requires participants to choose between two plausible analogies, which more closely mimics real-world complexity compared to studies that provide a single source analogy.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel experimental design:** The introduction of a "matching problem" with two source analogies and two target problems is a significant improvement over traditional analogical transfer studies. This enhances the ecological validity of the study.
*   **Direct comparison of humans and AI:** Directly comparing human and AI performance on the same task provides clear insights into their relative strengths and weaknesses.
*   **Identification of distinct error profiles:** The finding that GPT-4 relies on surface-level similarity while humans rely on causal reasoning is a crucial distinction.
*   **Practical implications:** The paper offers actionable recommendations for integrating AI into strategic decision-making, suggesting a division of labor where AI generates analogies and humans evaluate them.
*   **Emphasis on the evaluation/matching phase:** By focusing on the evaluation stage of analogical reasoning, the paper highlights a relatively neglected area of research, challenging existing models that mainly emphasize retrieval and mapping.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited generalizability:** The study uses business school students as human participants, which may limit the generalizability of the findings to senior executives or other populations.
*   **Single LLM:** The study only examines GPT-4, and the findings may not generalize to other LLMs with different architectures or training data.
*   **Simplified task environment:** While the "matching problem" is more realistic than traditional studies, the task environment is still simplified compared to real-world strategic decision-making, which involves far more information and complexity.
*   **Static Prompting approach for LLM:** The approach of static prompts towards LLM may limit the ability of the LLM to perform at its best, and dynamic engagement with iterative prompting could have led to different results.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper’s primary novelty lies in its empirical demonstration of the different error patterns exhibited by humans and LLMs during analogical reasoning for strategic decision-making. This is significant because it identifies a crucial gap in LLMs' capabilities – the ability to deeply understand and map causal structures compared to superficial surface similarities, as well as provides a potential framework for AI & human collaborations to overcome those weaknesses. While some research has explored analogical reasoning in AI, this paper specifically focuses on strategic problems and provides direct evidence of how humans and AI can complement each other. The identification of the "matching" stage as a crucial but often overlooked aspect of analogical reasoning also adds a valuable theoretical contribution.

The paper has the potential to influence future research by encouraging studies to investigate the human-AI collaboration framework & the effectiveness of hybrid approaches that combine the strengths of both. It also highlights the need for further research into the factors that influence human analogical reasoning in complex, real-world settings.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper deserves a score of 7.5 because it offers a valuable empirical contribution and practical recommendations for integrating AI into strategic decision-making. However, the limitations related to sample population, the usage of a single LLM, and a simplified task environment hold it back from a higher score. The innovative experimental design and the identification of distinct error profiles, the lack of a dynamic approach to prompting, and the emphasis on the evaluation/matching phase make it a solid piece of research that contributes to the growing understanding of AI and human cognition in strategic settings.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pixel3DMM: Versatile Screen-Space Priors for Single-Image 3D Face Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00615v1)
- **Authors**: Simon Giebenhain, Tobias Kirschstein, Martin Rünz, Lourdes Agapito, Matthias Nießner
- **Abstract**: We address the 3D reconstruction of human faces from a single RGB image. To this end, we propose Pixel3DMM, a set of highly-generalized vision transformers which predict per-pixel geometric cues in order to constrain the optimization of a 3D morphable face model (3DMM). We exploit the latent features of the DINO foundation model, and introduce a tailored surface normal and uv-coordinate prediction head. We train our model by registering three high-quality 3D face datasets against the FLAME mesh topology, which results in a total of over 1,000 identities and 976K images. For 3D face reconstruction, we propose a FLAME fitting opitmization that solves for the 3DMM parameters from the uv-coordinate and normal estimates. To evaluate our method, we introduce a new benchmark for single-image face reconstruction, which features high diversity facial expressions, viewing angles, and ethnicities. Crucially, our benchmark is the first to evaluate both posed and neutral facial geometry. Ultimately, our method outperforms the most competitive baselines by over 15% in terms of geometric accuracy for posed facial expressions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Pixel3DMM," a novel approach to single-image 3D face reconstruction. It leverages vision transformers (ViTs) to predict per-pixel surface normals and UV coordinates, which are then used as constraints in an optimization process to fit a FLAME 3D morphable model (3DMM). The method unifies multiple high-quality 3D face datasets through a registration process, creating a large and diverse training set. The authors also propose a new benchmark based on the NeRSemble dataset for evaluating single-image face reconstruction, covering a wide range of expressions and viewpoints.  The method is evaluated by outperforming existing baselines, especially for posed facial expressions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several existing ideas, but with tailored implementation choices that produce noticeable improvements. Using foundation model features (DINOv2) is becoming common, but the specific architecture for predicting surface normals and UV coordinates appears well-designed. The use of UV coordinates to inform vertex positions for loss calculation is also a reasonable choice to stabilize the fitting optimization process. The approach is not revolutionary, but represents a strong engineering effort to integrate recent advances effectively.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. While the performance gains are promising, especially on posed expressions, the overall architecture is built upon existing concepts. A notable contribution is the new benchmark dataset extracted from NeRSemble. The inclusion of both posed and neutral expression evaluations is a valuable addition, addressing a limitation in previous datasets. The public availability of the code, model and benchmark, if properly executed, would certainly push future work towards that direction.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Performance:** Demonstrates improvements over existing baselines, especially on posed expressions where other methods struggle.
    *   **Large and Diverse Dataset:** The unification of multiple datasets and their registration create a sizable training set.
    *   **New Benchmark:** Introduces a more comprehensive benchmark with diverse expressions and the inclusion of neutral geometry evaluation.
    *   **Clear Implementation Details:** Describes network architecture, training procedure, and optimization process reasonably well.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The core approach doesn't introduce fundamentally new concepts but rather combines and refines existing techniques.
    *   **Reliance on Foundation Models:** While leveraging foundation models is beneficial, it also makes the approach less original.
    *   **Optimization Bottleneck:** The relatively slow optimization process (30 seconds) limits real-time applicability.
    *   **Limited Insight into Disentanglement:** Claims improvements in disentanglement but only show metrics, leaving doubts in the understanding of the underlying processes.

*   **Potential Influence:** The proposed benchmark could have a positive impact by promoting more comprehensive evaluations of 3D face reconstruction methods, including a focus on expression disentanglement. The improved performance and publicly available code could also lead to the adoption of this approach as a component in other systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's combination of existing techniques with tailored implementations, the incremental nature of the contribution, and the moderate performance improvements, a score in the middle range seems appropriate. The new benchmark and the thorough experimental evaluation are positive aspects that boost the score. The primary innovation is in the careful combination of existing technologies and a strong engineering effort.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### FineScope : Precision Pruning for Domain-Specialized Large Language Models Using SAE-Guided Self-Data Cultivation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00624v1)
- **Authors**: Chaitali Bhattacharyya, Yeseong Kim
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) from scratch requires significant computational resources, driving interest in developing smaller, domain-specific LLMs that maintain both efficiency and strong task performance. Medium-sized models such as LLaMA, llama} have served as starting points for domain-specific adaptation, but they often suffer from accuracy degradation when tested on specialized datasets. We introduce FineScope, a framework for deriving compact, domain-optimized LLMs from larger pretrained models.
